People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you, um, thanks for seeing me. I just... I don't really know where to start, to be honest. I guess what brings me in is that things have just become completely unmanageable lately. I work at a church, I'm on the pastoral staff, and, you know, you're supposed to be the one who has it together for everyone else, the one people come to for peace. But I'll just be standing there in the church office, literally just trying to act normal, maybe sorting through some emails or preparing a study, and I just keep getting hit out of absolutely nowhere with it. 

It’s... it's this scene of my cousin. Just lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it, it's like I'm dragged right back into it. I can hear all the shouting all over again, so loud and chaotic, and I can see the lights flashing, just completely blinding me, even though I know I'm just standing in a quiet room on a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything. I try to push it away because I have to be present for my congregation, but it's so vivid and it just hijacks my brain completely, and I just can't keep living like this where I'm constantly being pulled back to that pavement.

Most days, honestly almost every single day, I feel this horrible, heavy mix of fear, anger, and just this crushing guilt, and it's just sitting right there in the middle of my chest from the exact minute I wake up. Before my feet even hit the floor in the morning, it’s already there. 

And it's so complicated to explain because... well, because of my calling, you know? I'm supposed to be a man of faith. I'm supposed to trust in a higher plan and lead others to that trust. But instead, I just feel so incredibly angry about what happened, and then I immediately feel guilty for being angry. It’s this endless cycle. I just feel like... like I failed everyone. I feel like I completely failed God, which is a terrifying thing for a pastor to admit out loud. I feel like I failed my family, who always expect me to be the spiritual rock, the strong one who can pray us through any tragedy. And... and the kids at the church, the youth group kids who trusted me to have the answers and to be this safe, stable presence... I feel like I'm failing them every single time I stand in front of them because inside I'm just eaten alive by this guilt and this fear. I try to pray it away, I really do, I spend hours in my office just trying to give it over, but that heavy feeling in my chest just doesn't leave me alone, no matter what I do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I mean... it's not just a general feeling of not being good enough, it's... it's very specific. It's this loop playing in my head constantly, telling me exactly how I failed. I mean, I'm the older one, you know? I'm the one who's supposed to be responsible. And I keep going over that night, over and over and over again, dissecting every single decision I made. 

I just keep telling myself... I keep thinking that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, you know? We were having a good time, the kids were laughing, and I didn't want to cut it short because it's so rare to see them all just getting along and being kids without all the usual drama. But if I had just looked at the clock and said, "Okay, wrap it up," then we would have left before everything happened. Or... or if I had just walked him out to his car. Why didn't I just walk him out? It would have taken two minutes. I was right there by the door, talking to some parents, and I just let him walk out into the parking lot alone. 

And those boys... the ones who were hanging around the edge of the property earlier... I saw them. I noticed them, and I should have kept a better watch over them. I should have gone out there and told them to move along, or at least kept my eye on them until everyone was safely gone. But I got distracted. I was talking, I was being the pastor, shaking hands and making small talk, and I just... I let my guard down. And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.

I feel like I'm failing them every single time I stand in front of them because inside I'm just eaten alive by this guilt and this fear. I try to pray it away, I really do, I spend hours in my office just trying to give it over, but that heavy feeling in my chest just doesn't leave me alone, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re a failure, even outside of these memories and feelings of guilt?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's not just a general feeling of not being good enough, it's... it's very specific. It's this loop playing in my head constantly, telling me exactly how I failed. I mean, I'm the older one, you know? I'm the one who's supposed to be responsible. And I keep going over that night, over and over and over again, dissecting every single decision I made. \n\nI just keep telling myself... I keep thinking that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, you know? We were having a good time, the kids were laughing, and I didn't want to cut it short because it's so rare to see them all just getting along and being kids without all the usual drama. But if I had just looked at the clock and said, \"Okay, wrap it up,\" then we would have left before everything happened. Or... or if I had just walked him out to his car. Why didn't I just walk him out? It would have taken two minutes. I was right there by the door, talking to some parents, and I just let him walk out into the parking lot alone. \n\nAnd those boys... the ones who were hanging around the edge of the property earlier... I saw them. I noticed them, and I should have kept a better watch over them. I should have gone out there and told them to move along, or at least kept my eye on them until everyone was safely gone. But I got distracted. I was talking, I was being the pastor, shaking hands and making small talk, and I just... I let my guard down. And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, I absolutely blame myself. Completely. And the thing is, people keep using that exact phrase with me—my senior pastor, my parents, the police—they all keep telling me, "It was out of your control, you couldn't have known." But I just don't see it that way. I don't think it was out of my control at all, because I was the one in charge of the entire evening. I was the adult in the room. 

I just keep telling myself, over and over, every single hour of the day, that if I had just ended the event earlier... I mean, we were playing this goofy icebreaker game in the fellowship hall, and it ran maybe fifteen minutes long. Fifteen minutes. If I had just looked at my watch and said, "Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys," the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. 

And then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. 

And those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. 

If I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.

I feel like I failed my family, who always expect me to be the spiritual rock, the strong one who can pray us through any tragedy. I feel like I'm failing them every single time I stand in front of them because inside I'm just eaten alive by this guilt and this fear. I try to pray it away, I really do, I spend hours in my office just trying to give it over, but that heavy feeling in my chest just doesn't leave me alone, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re a failure, even outside of these memories and feelings of guilt?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's not just a general feeling of not being good enough, it's... it's very specific. It's this loop playing in my head constantly, telling me exactly how I failed. I mean, I'm the older one, you know? I'm the one who's supposed to be responsible. And I keep going over that night, over and over and over again, dissecting every single decision I made. \n\nI just keep telling myself... I keep thinking that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, you know? We were having a good time, the kids were laughing, and I didn't want to cut it short because it's so rare to see them all just getting along and being kids without all the usual drama. But if I had just looked at the clock and said, \"Okay, wrap it up,\" then we would have left before everything happened. Or... or if I had just walked him out to his car. Why didn't I just walk him out? It would have taken two minutes. I was right there by the door, talking to some parents, and I just let him walk out into the parking lot alone. \n\nAnd those boys... the ones who were hanging around the edge of the property earlier... I saw them. I noticed them, and I should have kept a better watch over them. I should have gone out there and told them to move along, or at least kept my eye on them until everyone was safely gone. But I got distracted. I was talking, I was being the pastor, shaking hands and making small talk, and I just... I let my guard down. And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself blaming yourself for the event, or feeling responsible for things that happened that were out of your control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, I absolutely blame myself. Completely. And the thing is, people keep using that exact phrase with me—my senior pastor, my parents, the police—they all keep telling me, \"It was out of your control, you couldn't have known.\" But I just don't see it that way. I don't think it was out of my control at all, because I was the one in charge of the entire evening. I was the adult in the room. \n\nI just keep telling myself, over and over, every single hour of the day, that if I had just ended the event earlier... I mean, we were playing this goofy icebreaker game in the fellowship hall, and it ran maybe fifteen minutes long. Fifteen minutes. If I had just looked at my watch and said, \"Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys,\" the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. \n\nAnd then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. \n\nAnd those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. \n\nIf I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been experiencing flashbacks, nightmares, or intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s death that make you feel like you’re reliving the event?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / clergy / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep getting hit out of nowhere with the scene of my cousin on that pavement, like I can hear the shouting and see the lights again even when I'm standing in the church office trying to act normal.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yes. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you in the first place. It’s just... it’s completely overwhelming. I keep getting hit, just completely out of nowhere, with this... this scene of my cousin lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it like a normal, bad memory, you know? It's like I am physically ripped right back into that exact second in time. 

I can hear the shouting all over again—the exact pitch of the voices, the chaos, the panic—and I can see those police lights flashing, just bouncing off the brick on the side of the fellowship hall. It's so vivid. And the craziest part is, it doesn't even just happen when I'm alone at night or something. I could literally just be standing in the church office. Just standing there by the desk, trying to act completely normal, maybe looking over a scripture reading for Sunday or talking to the church secretary about the bulletin, and then suddenly... the office is just gone. The present is gone. I'm right back on that pavement, seeing him there, hearing the shouting, seeing those lights. 

I try so hard to just blink it away and keep a straight face, because people are looking at me and I'm supposed to be their pastor, I have to hold it together and be the strong one. But I'm just reliving that horrible, horrible night all over again, right there in the middle of a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, I really have. And it’s... it’s honestly so out of character for me, but lately, it’s just like I have absolutely zero patience. Zero. My fuse is just completely gone. I’ve actually been snapping in our ministry meetings over completely stupid, small things. 

Like, just last week we were in a planning committee for the upcoming youth retreat, just a standard Tuesday night meeting with the deacons and some of my volunteer youth leaders. And someone asked a completely innocent question about the transportation schedule, or maybe the budget for snacks, I can't even remember what it was, but I just... I completely lost it. I raised my voice at them. I was practically yelling at these deacons and youth leaders, people who are just volunteering their free time to help me, and I was talking to them like they were the enemy. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. 

The whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I mean, I'm the pastor, I'm supposed to be the calm, steady one, the one who brings peace to the room. And afterward... man, I just hate how fast I went there. I hate it so much. It was like going from completely fine to absolute rage in a fraction of a second. I had to go sit in my office after they left and just put my head in my hands, because I don't even know where this anger is coming from. It’s like my nerves are just completely raw, and the slightest bit of pushback or confusion just sets me off. I’ve had to apologize so many times lately, and I just know they have to be looking at me differently now, wondering why their youth pastor is suddenly acting so hostile over a simple logistical question. It just feels like I can't stop the anger before it spills out.

If I had just looked at my watch and said, \"Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys,\" the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. \n\nAnd then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. \n\nAnd those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. \n\nIf I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing flashbacks, nightmares, or intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s death that make you feel like you’re reliving the event?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yes. I keep getting hit, just completely out of nowhere, with this... this scene of my cousin lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it like a normal, bad memory, you know? It's like I am physically ripped right back into that exact second in time. \n\nI can hear the shouting all over again—the exact pitch of the voices, the chaos, the panic—and I can see those police lights flashing, just bouncing off the brick on the side of the fellowship hall. The present is gone. I'm right back on that pavement, seeing him there, hearing the shouting, seeing those lights. \n\nI try so hard to just blink it away and keep a straight face, because people are looking at me and I'm supposed to be their pastor, I have to hold it together and be the strong one. But I'm just reliving that horrible, horrible night all over again, right there in the middle of a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling especially irritable or having outbursts of anger lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I really have. And it’s... it’s honestly so out of character for me, but lately, it’s just like I have absolutely zero patience. Zero. My fuse is just completely gone. I’ve actually been snapping in our ministry meetings over completely stupid, small things. \n\nLike, just last week we were in a planning committee for the upcoming youth retreat, just a standard Tuesday night meeting with the deacons and some of my volunteer youth leaders. And someone asked a completely innocent question about the transportation schedule, or maybe the budget for snacks, I can't even remember what it was, but I just... I completely lost it. I raised my voice at them. I was practically yelling at these deacons and youth leaders, people who are just volunteering their free time to help me, and I was talking to them like they were the enemy. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. \n\nThe whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I mean, I'm the pastor, I'm supposed to be the calm, steady one, the one who brings peace to the room. And afterward... man, I just hate how fast I went there. I hate it so much. It was like going from completely fine to absolute rage in a fraction of a second. I had to go sit in my office after they left and just put my head in my hands, because I don't even know where this anger is coming from. It’s like my nerves are just completely raw, and the slightest bit of pushback or confusion just sets me off. I’ve had to apologize so many times lately, and I just know they have to be looking at me differently now, wondering why their youth pastor is suddenly acting so hostile over a simple logistical question. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling especially irritable or having outbursts of anger lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I really have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... it’s like everything just lost its color completely. You brought up preparing sermons, and... man, I used to love preaching. I really did. I would spend hours on a Wednesday night just digging into the text, trying to find the perfect illustration, getting so fired up to share it with the youth group. It was my absolute favorite thing. But now? I just stare at my laptop screen. I type a few bullet points and I just... I mostly just go through the motions. I stand up there and say the words, but there's nothing behind them. 

And it’s not just the formal ministry stuff, either. It’s literally everything. Like, I’ve played the keys since I was ten years old. I used to get up there with the worship band on Sunday mornings, or even just sit in the empty sanctuary on a Thursday afternoon and just play for hours. Just me and the piano, feeling so connected to God and to the music. Now, I don't even want to look at the keyboard. I've been asking the other worship leader to cover all my sets because I just can't bring myself to press the keys. 

Same with taking pictures. I used to always have my camera around my neck, always snapping candids of the kids, the retreats, the baptisms... I loved capturing those moments. But my camera has just been sitting in its bag in my office since that night. I haven't touched it once. 

And... and even just hanging out. We used to have this pickup game, running with the guys on the court behind the church. It was my favorite way to blow off steam and connect with the older teens outside of a formal church setting. We'd play until the streetlights came on, just laughing and talking trash. But now, when they text me to come out and play, I just make up an excuse. I avoid it altogether. I avoid all of it. 

I don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I'm just a ghost walking through the halls of that church, doing what I'm supposed to do because I'm on staff, but the joy, the passion... it's just completely gone. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.

And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself blaming yourself for the event, or feeling responsible for things that happened that were out of your control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, I absolutely blame myself. Completely. And the thing is, people keep using that exact phrase with me—my senior pastor, my parents, the police—they all keep telling me, \"It was out of your control, you couldn't have known.\" But I just don't see it that way. I don't think it was out of my control at all, because I was the one in charge of the entire evening. I was the adult in the room. \n\nI just keep telling myself, over and over, every single hour of the day, that if I had just ended the event earlier... I mean, we were playing this goofy icebreaker game in the fellowship hall, and it ran maybe fifteen minutes long. Fifteen minutes. If I had just looked at my watch and said, \"Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys,\" the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. \n\nAnd then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. \n\nAnd those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. \n\nIf I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing flashbacks, nightmares, or intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s death that make you feel like you’re reliving the event?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yes. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you in the first place. It’s just... it’s completely overwhelming. I keep getting hit, just completely out of nowhere, with this... this scene of my cousin lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it like a normal, bad memory, you know? It's like I am physically ripped right back into that exact second in time. \n\nI can hear the shouting all over again—the exact pitch of the voices, the chaos, the panic—and I can see those police lights flashing, just bouncing off the brick on the side of the fellowship hall. It's so vivid. And the craziest part is, it doesn't even just happen when I'm alone at night or something. I could literally just be standing in the church office. Just standing there by the desk, trying to act completely normal, maybe looking over a scripture reading for Sunday or talking to the church secretary about the bulletin, and then suddenly... the office is just gone. The present is gone. I'm right back on that pavement, seeing him there, hearing the shouting, seeing those lights. \n\nI try so hard to just blink it away and keep a straight face, because people are looking at me and I'm supposed to be their pastor, I have to hold it together and be the strong one. But I'm just reliving that horrible, horrible night all over again, right there in the middle of a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling especially irritable or having outbursts of anger lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I really have. And it’s... it’s honestly so out of character for me, but lately, it’s just like I have absolutely zero patience. Zero. My fuse is just completely gone. I’ve actually been snapping in our ministry meetings over completely stupid, small things. \n\nLike, just last week we were in a planning committee for the upcoming youth retreat, just a standard Tuesday night meeting with the deacons and some of my volunteer youth leaders. And someone asked a completely innocent question about the transportation schedule, or maybe the budget for snacks, I can't even remember what it was, but I just... I completely lost it. I raised my voice at them. I was practically yelling at these deacons and youth leaders, people who are just volunteering their free time to help me, and I was talking to them like they were the enemy. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. \n\nThe whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I mean, I'm the pastor, I'm supposed to be the calm, steady one, the one who brings peace to the room. And afterward... man, I just hate how fast I went there. I hate it so much. It was like going from completely fine to absolute rage in a fraction of a second. I had to go sit in my office after they left and just put my head in my hands, because I don't even know where this anger is coming from. It’s like my nerves are just completely raw, and the slightest bit of pushback or confusion just sets me off. I’ve had to apologize so many times lately, and I just know they have to be looking at me differently now, wondering why their youth pastor is suddenly acting so hostile over a simple logistical question. It just feels like I can't stop the anger before it spills out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that used to be meaningful to you, like leading your youth group or preparing your sermons?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it’s like everything just lost its color completely. You brought up preparing sermons, and... man, I used to love preaching. I really did. I would spend hours on a Wednesday night just digging into the text, trying to find the perfect illustration, getting so fired up to share it with the youth group. It was my absolute favorite thing. But now? I just stare at my laptop screen. I type a few bullet points and I just... I mostly just go through the motions. I stand up there and say the words, but there's nothing behind them. \n\nAnd it’s not just the formal ministry stuff, either. It’s literally everything. Like, I’ve played the keys since I was ten years old. I used to get up there with the worship band on Sunday mornings, or even just sit in the empty sanctuary on a Thursday afternoon and just play for hours. Just me and the piano, feeling so connected to God and to the music. Now, I don't even want to look at the keyboard. I've been asking the other worship leader to cover all my sets because I just can't bring myself to press the keys. \n\nSame with taking pictures. I used to always have my camera around my neck, always snapping candids of the kids, the retreats, the baptisms... I loved capturing those moments. But my camera has just been sitting in its bag in my office since that night. I haven't touched it once. \n\nAnd... and even just hanging out. We used to have this pickup game, running with the guys on the court behind the church. It was my favorite way to blow off steam and connect with the older teens outside of a formal church setting. We'd play until the streetlights came on, just laughing and talking trash. But now, when they text me to come out and play, I just make up an excuse. I avoid it altogether. I avoid all of it. \n\nI don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I'm just a ghost walking through the halls of that church, doing what I'm supposed to do because I'm on staff, but the joy, the passion... it's just completely gone. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. It's... it's honestly terrifying how much my brain just feels like it's full of thick fog right now. I was actually just thinking about this the other day because it’s becoming a real liability for my job. 

When I sit down to write a sermon... well, I mentioned earlier how much I used to love doing that, right? I used to be able to just dive in, connect all these different theological concepts, pull from my undergrad classes, and just outline a message with no problem. But now? I sit there at my desk in the church office, I open up my laptop, and my mind just completely blanks out. It’s like someone literally just pulled the plug on my brain. There is just nothing there. Just static. 

And it’s not even just the heavy, deep stuff that takes a lot of mental energy, either. It’s the incredibly simple, mundane things. Like just answering church emails. I’ll open my inbox, and there will be a message from one of the youth parents asking a basic question about the permission slips for the fall retreat, or maybe an email from the senior pastor about the order of service. Just totally standard stuff that I've handled a thousand times since I got ordained. But I’ll open the email, and I will read the first sentence... and then I’ll realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. 

So I go back. I read it again. And then I read it again. I will literally sit there and read the exact same line five or six times in a row, and I am just not taking in a single thing it says. I see the letters, I know they form words, but the meaning just doesn't register in my head at all. It’s incredibly frustrating. I used to be so sharp, you know? I could juggle the youth group schedule, volunteer coordination, counseling sessions, all of it, and never drop a ball. Now, I can barely figure out how to reply to an email about a pizza party without my brain hitting a massive brick wall. I just sit there staring at the blinking cursor, totally paralyzed, unable to form a single coherent thought or make a basic decision about what to type back. It takes me an hour to do what used to take me two minutes, and it is just completely exhausting trying to force my brain to work through this sludge every single day.

I raised my voice at them. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. \n\nThe whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I avoid all of it. \n\nI don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder for you to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. We used to have this pickup game, running with the guys on the court behind the church. It was my favorite way to blow off steam and connect with the older teens outside of a formal church setting. We'd play until the streetlights came on, just laughing and talking trash. But now, when they text me to come out and play, I just make up an excuse. I avoid it altogether. I avoid all of it. \n\nI don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I'm just a ghost walking through the halls of that church, doing what I'm supposed to do because I'm on staff, but the joy, the passion... it's just completely gone. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder for you to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's... it's honestly terrifying how much my brain just feels like it's full of thick fog right now. I was actually just thinking about this the other day because it’s becoming a real liability for my job. \n\nWhen I sit down to write a sermon... well, I mentioned earlier how much I used to love doing that, right? I used to be able to just dive in, connect all these different theological concepts, pull from my undergrad classes, and just outline a message with no problem. But now? I sit there at my desk in the church office, I open up my laptop, and my mind just completely blanks out. It’s like someone literally just pulled the plug on my brain. There is just nothing there. Just static. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the heavy, deep stuff that takes a lot of mental energy, either. It’s the incredibly simple, mundane things. Like just answering church emails. I’ll open my inbox, and there will be a message from one of the youth parents asking a basic question about the permission slips for the fall retreat, or maybe an email from the senior pastor about the order of service. Just totally standard stuff that I've handled a thousand times since I got ordained. But I’ll open the email, and I will read the first sentence... and then I’ll realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. \n\nSo I go back. I read it again. And then I read it again. I will literally sit there and read the exact same line five or six times in a row, and I am just not taking in a single thing it says. I see the letters, I know they form words, but the meaning just doesn't register in my head at all. It’s incredibly frustrating. I used to be so sharp, you know? I could juggle the youth group schedule, volunteer coordination, counseling sessions, all of it, and never drop a ball. Now, I can barely figure out how to reply to an email about a pizza party without my brain hitting a massive brick wall. I just sit there staring at the blinking cursor, totally paralyzed, unable to form a single coherent thought or make a basic decision about what to type back. It just takes over everything. I try to push it away because I have to be present for my congregation, but it's so vivid and it just hijacks my brain completely, and I just can't keep living like this where I'm constantly being pulled back to that pavement.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what emotions come up for you when you're pulled back to that scene with your cousin?\n\nPatient: It’s... well, when I’m pulled back there, it’s just this overwhelming flood, but honestly, it’s not even just when the memories hit anymore. It’s... it's constant. Most days, honestly almost every single day, I feel this horrible, heavy mix of fear, anger, and just this crushing guilt, and it's just sitting right there in the middle of my chest from the exact minute I wake up. Before my feet even hit the floor in the morning, it’s already there. \n\nAnd it's so complicated to explain because... well, because of my calling, you know? I'm supposed to be a man of faith. I'm supposed to trust in a higher plan and lead others to that trust. But instead, I just feel so incredibly angry about what happened, and then I immediately feel guilty for being angry. It’s this endless cycle. I just feel like... like I failed everyone. I feel like I completely failed God, which is a terrifying thing for a pastor to admit out loud. I feel like I failed my family, who always expect me to be the spiritual rock, the strong one who can pray us through any tragedy. And... and the kids at the church, the youth group kids who trusted me to have the answers and to be this safe, stable presence... I feel like I'm failing them every single time I stand in front of them because inside I'm just eaten alive by this guilt and this fear. I try to pray it away, I really do, I spend hours in my office just trying to give it over, but that heavy feeling in my chest just doesn't leave me alone, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re a failure, even outside of these memories and feelings of guilt?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's not just a general feeling of not being good enough, it's... it's very specific. It's this loop playing in my head constantly, telling me exactly how I failed. I mean, I'm the older one, you know? I'm the one who's supposed to be responsible. And I keep going over that night, over and over and over again, dissecting every single decision I made. \n\nI just keep telling myself... I keep thinking that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, you know? We were having a good time, the kids were laughing, and I didn't want to cut it short because it's so rare to see them all just getting along and being kids without all the usual drama. But if I had just looked at the clock and said, \"Okay, wrap it up,\" then we would have left before everything happened. Or... or if I had just walked him out to his car. Why didn't I just walk him out? It would have taken two minutes. I was right there by the door, talking to some parents, and I just let him walk out into the parking lot alone. \n\nAnd those boys... the ones who were hanging around the edge of the property earlier... I saw them. I noticed them, and I should have kept a better watch over them. I should have gone out there and told them to move along, or at least kept my eye on them until everyone was safely gone. But I got distracted. I was talking, I was being the pastor, shaking hands and making small talk, and I just... I let my guard down. And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself blaming yourself for the event, or feeling responsible for things that happened that were out of your control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, I absolutely blame myself. Completely. And the thing is, people keep using that exact phrase with me—my senior pastor, my parents, the police—they all keep telling me, \"It was out of your control, you couldn't have known.\" But I just don't see it that way. I don't think it was out of my control at all, because I was the one in charge of the entire evening. I was the adult in the room. \n\nI just keep telling myself, over and over, every single hour of the day, that if I had just ended the event earlier... I mean, we were playing this goofy icebreaker game in the fellowship hall, and it ran maybe fifteen minutes long. Fifteen minutes. If I had just looked at my watch and said, \"Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys,\" the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. \n\nAnd then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. \n\nAnd those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. \n\nIf I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing flashbacks, nightmares, or intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s death that make you feel like you’re reliving the event?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yes. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you in the first place. It’s just... it’s completely overwhelming. I keep getting hit, just completely out of nowhere, with this... this scene of my cousin lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it like a normal, bad memory, you know? It's like I am physically ripped right back into that exact second in time. \n\nI can hear the shouting all over again—the exact pitch of the voices, the chaos, the panic—and I can see those police lights flashing, just bouncing off the brick on the side of the fellowship hall. It's so vivid. And the craziest part is, it doesn't even just happen when I'm alone at night or something. I could literally just be standing in the church office. Just standing there by the desk, trying to act completely normal, maybe looking over a scripture reading for Sunday or talking to the church secretary about the bulletin, and then suddenly... the office is just gone. The present is gone. I'm right back on that pavement, seeing him there, hearing the shouting, seeing those lights. \n\nI try so hard to just blink it away and keep a straight face, because people are looking at me and I'm supposed to be their pastor, I have to hold it together and be the strong one. But I'm just reliving that horrible, horrible night all over again, right there in the middle of a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling especially irritable or having outbursts of anger lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I really have. And it’s... it’s honestly so out of character for me, but lately, it’s just like I have absolutely zero patience. Zero. My fuse is just completely gone. I’ve actually been snapping in our ministry meetings over completely stupid, small things. \n\nLike, just last week we were in a planning committee for the upcoming youth retreat, just a standard Tuesday night meeting with the deacons and some of my volunteer youth leaders. And someone asked a completely innocent question about the transportation schedule, or maybe the budget for snacks, I can't even remember what it was, but I just... I completely lost it. I raised my voice at them. I was practically yelling at these deacons and youth leaders, people who are just volunteering their free time to help me, and I was talking to them like they were the enemy. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. \n\nThe whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I mean, I'm the pastor, I'm supposed to be the calm, steady one, the one who brings peace to the room. And afterward... man, I just hate how fast I went there. I hate it so much. It was like going from completely fine to absolute rage in a fraction of a second. I had to go sit in my office after they left and just put my head in my hands, because I don't even know where this anger is coming from. It’s like my nerves are just completely raw, and the slightest bit of pushback or confusion just sets me off. I’ve had to apologize so many times lately, and I just know they have to be looking at me differently now, wondering why their youth pastor is suddenly acting so hostile over a simple logistical question. It just feels like I can't stop the anger before it spills out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that used to be meaningful to you, like leading your youth group or preparing your sermons?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it’s like everything just lost its color completely. You brought up preparing sermons, and... man, I used to love preaching. I really did. I would spend hours on a Wednesday night just digging into the text, trying to find the perfect illustration, getting so fired up to share it with the youth group. It was my absolute favorite thing. But now? I just stare at my laptop screen. I type a few bullet points and I just... I mostly just go through the motions. I stand up there and say the words, but there's nothing behind them. \n\nAnd it’s not just the formal ministry stuff, either. It’s literally everything. Like, I’ve played the keys since I was ten years old. I used to get up there with the worship band on Sunday mornings, or even just sit in the empty sanctuary on a Thursday afternoon and just play for hours. Just me and the piano, feeling so connected to God and to the music. Now, I don't even want to look at the keyboard. I've been asking the other worship leader to cover all my sets because I just can't bring myself to press the keys. \n\nSame with taking pictures. I used to always have my camera around my neck, always snapping candids of the kids, the retreats, the baptisms... I loved capturing those moments. But my camera has just been sitting in its bag in my office since that night. I haven't touched it once. \n\nAnd... and even just hanging out. We used to have this pickup game, running with the guys on the court behind the church. It was my favorite way to blow off steam and connect with the older teens outside of a formal church setting. We'd play until the streetlights came on, just laughing and talking trash. But now, when they text me to come out and play, I just make up an excuse. I avoid it altogether. I avoid all of it. \n\nI don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I'm just a ghost walking through the halls of that church, doing what I'm supposed to do because I'm on staff, but the joy, the passion... it's just completely gone. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder for you to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's... it's honestly terrifying how much my brain just feels like it's full of thick fog right now. I was actually just thinking about this the other day because it’s becoming a real liability for my job. \n\nWhen I sit down to write a sermon... well, I mentioned earlier how much I used to love doing that, right? I used to be able to just dive in, connect all these different theological concepts, pull from my undergrad classes, and just outline a message with no problem. But now? I sit there at my desk in the church office, I open up my laptop, and my mind just completely blanks out. It’s like someone literally just pulled the plug on my brain. There is just nothing there. Just static. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the heavy, deep stuff that takes a lot of mental energy, either. It’s the incredibly simple, mundane things. Like just answering church emails. I’ll open my inbox, and there will be a message from one of the youth parents asking a basic question about the permission slips for the fall retreat, or maybe an email from the senior pastor about the order of service. Just totally standard stuff that I've handled a thousand times since I got ordained. But I’ll open the email, and I will read the first sentence... and then I’ll realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. \n\nSo I go back. I read it again. And then I read it again. I will literally sit there and read the exact same line five or six times in a row, and I am just not taking in a single thing it says. I see the letters, I know they form words, but the meaning just doesn't register in my head at all. It’s incredibly frustrating. I used to be so sharp, you know? I could juggle the youth group schedule, volunteer coordination, counseling sessions, all of it, and never drop a ball. Now, I can barely figure out how to reply to an email about a pizza party without my brain hitting a massive brick wall. I just sit there staring at the blinking cursor, totally paralyzed, unable to form a single coherent thought or make a basic decision about what to type back. It takes me an hour to do what used to take me two minutes, and it is just completely exhausting trying to force my brain to work through this sludge every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even things that used to be really meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I think I was actually just talking about this a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely. It's exactly like I was saying. I used to just completely love all of it. Preaching on a Sunday or Wednesday, playing the keys with the worship band, taking pictures of the kids at our youth events, or even just running with the guys on the basketball court out back... those were the things that gave me life, you know? Those were the things that made me feel connected to my calling and to the people around me. \n\nBut now, I mostly just go through the motions with the stuff I'm strictly obligated to do for my job, or I just avoid it altogether. I'll just make up excuses to get out of it. Because... well, like I said earlier, none of it feels alive in me anymore. I can sit down at the piano, or I can look at my camera sitting in its bag, or I can hear the guys playing basketball outside my office window, and there is just zero spark. Nothing. It's like the part of my soul that used to connect with those things, the part that used to feel joy and passion, is just completely numb now. I'll just stare at my sermon notes or my keyboard and just feel this heavy, dead weight in my chest. It's incredibly isolating, honestly, because my whole life was built around these things, and now I just feel completely disconnected from all of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I... Doc, I feel like we keep circling back to this, and I don't know how else to explain it, but yes. Yes, absolutely. Like I was just saying a minute ago, and even before that... it's all just gone. The youth group, the sermons, all of it.

But now? Now I just... I mostly just go through the motions if I absolutely have to for my job, or I just avoid it altogether. I dodge the texts for the pickup games. I leave my camera zipped up in its bag. I beg the other worship leader to play the piano for me. Because none of it feels alive in me anymore. It's just... it's like a void. I don't know how many different ways I can say it, but I'm just an empty shell doing the bare minimum to not get fired, and it's terrifying because I don't know if that spark is ever going to come back. I keep trying to force it, trying to sit at the keyboard or open my sermon document, and it's just dead. Completely dead.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.11, F32.3, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I’m Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what’s been bringing you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you, um, thanks for seeing me. I just... I don't really know where to start, to be honest. I guess what brings me in is that things have just become completely unmanageable lately. I work at a church, I'm on the pastoral staff, and, you know, you're supposed to be the one who has it together for everyone else, the one people come to for peace. But I'll just be standing there in the church office, literally just trying to act normal, maybe sorting through some emails or preparing a study, and I just keep getting hit out of absolutely nowhere with it. \n\nIt’s... it's this scene of my cousin. Just lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it, it's like I'm dragged right back into it. I can hear all the shouting all over again, so loud and chaotic, and I can see the lights flashing, just completely blinding me, even though I know I'm just standing in a quiet room on a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything. I try to push it away because I have to be present for my congregation, but it's so vivid and it just hijacks my brain completely, and I just can't keep living like this where I'm constantly being pulled back to that pavement.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what emotions come up for you when you're pulled back to that scene with your cousin?\n\nPatient: It’s... well, when I’m pulled back there, it’s just this overwhelming flood, but honestly, it’s not even just when the memories hit anymore. It’s... it's constant. Most days, honestly almost every single day, I feel this horrible, heavy mix of fear, anger, and just this crushing guilt, and it's just sitting right there in the middle of my chest from the exact minute I wake up. Before my feet even hit the floor in the morning, it’s already there. \n\nAnd it's so complicated to explain because... well, because of my calling, you know? I'm supposed to be a man of faith. I'm supposed to trust in a higher plan and lead others to that trust. But instead, I just feel so incredibly angry about what happened, and then I immediately feel guilty for being angry. It’s this endless cycle. I just feel like... like I failed everyone. I feel like I completely failed God, which is a terrifying thing for a pastor to admit out loud. I feel like I failed my family, who always expect me to be the spiritual rock, the strong one who can pray us through any tragedy. And... and the kids at the church, the youth group kids who trusted me to have the answers and to be this safe, stable presence... I feel like I'm failing them every single time I stand in front of them because inside I'm just eaten alive by this guilt and this fear. I try to pray it away, I really do, I spend hours in my office just trying to give it over, but that heavy feeling in my chest just doesn't leave me alone, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough, or that you’re a failure, even outside of these memories and feelings of guilt?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... it's not just a general feeling of not being good enough, it's... it's very specific. It's this loop playing in my head constantly, telling me exactly how I failed. I mean, I'm the older one, you know? I'm the one who's supposed to be responsible. And I keep going over that night, over and over and over again, dissecting every single decision I made. \n\nI just keep telling myself... I keep thinking that if I had just ended the youth event earlier, you know? We were having a good time, the kids were laughing, and I didn't want to cut it short because it's so rare to see them all just getting along and being kids without all the usual drama. But if I had just looked at the clock and said, \"Okay, wrap it up,\" then we would have left before everything happened. Or... or if I had just walked him out to his car. Why didn't I just walk him out? It would have taken two minutes. I was right there by the door, talking to some parents, and I just let him walk out into the parking lot alone. \n\nAnd those boys... the ones who were hanging around the edge of the property earlier... I saw them. I noticed them, and I should have kept a better watch over them. I should have gone out there and told them to move along, or at least kept my eye on them until everyone was safely gone. But I got distracted. I was talking, I was being the pastor, shaking hands and making small talk, and I just... I let my guard down. And because I did that, because I didn't do what I was supposed to do, my cousin is gone. I just keep thinking that if I had done any of those things differently—any single one of them—he would still be here. He'd still be alive. And how do you ever forgive yourself for that? How do you stand up on a Sunday morning and talk about grace and forgiveness when you know, deep down, that you're the reason your own cousin is dead? It just eats away at me, every single second of the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself blaming yourself for the event, or feeling responsible for things that happened that were out of your control?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... like I was just saying, I absolutely blame myself. Completely. And the thing is, people keep using that exact phrase with me—my senior pastor, my parents, the police—they all keep telling me, \"It was out of your control, you couldn't have known.\" But I just don't see it that way. I don't think it was out of my control at all, because I was the one in charge of the entire evening. I was the adult in the room. \n\nI just keep telling myself, over and over, every single hour of the day, that if I had just ended the event earlier... I mean, we were playing this goofy icebreaker game in the fellowship hall, and it ran maybe fifteen minutes long. Fifteen minutes. If I had just looked at my watch and said, \"Okay, that's it, time to go home, guys,\" the timeline would have shifted. He wouldn't have been out in that parking lot at that exact minute. The intersection of those events wouldn't have happened. \n\nAnd then... if I had just walked him out. I'm the youth pastor, but I'm also his older cousin. It's my job to protect him, you know? The glass doors to the fellowship hall are maybe fifty feet from where he parked his car. Fifty feet! If I had just walked out those doors with him, just stood there on the sidewalk while he unlocked his car, none of this would have happened. They wouldn't have approached him if there was an adult standing right there. I know they wouldn't have. \n\nAnd those boys... I saw them. That's the part that really just destroys me. I keep telling myself that if I had kept a better watch over those boys... I saw them hanging around the edge of the church property, over near the dumpsters by the alleyway. I knew they didn't belong to our youth group. I even had this gut feeling, this tiny little voice telling me to go check it out, but I ignored it because I was busy putting away folding chairs and talking to one of the parents about the upcoming Sunday service. I prioritized making small talk over going out there and telling those kids to move along, or at least keeping my eyes glued to them until every single one of my youth group kids drove away safely. \n\nIf I had just done one of those things. Just one. Ended it earlier, walked him out, or watched those boys... my cousin would still be here. He'd be applying to colleges right now. He'd be sitting in the second row on Sundays like he always did. It wasn't out of my control. I had all the opportunities in the world to change how that night ended, and I just... I fumbled every single one of them. So, yes, I blame myself. I don't know how I could ever not blame myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing flashbacks, nightmares, or intrusive thoughts about your cousin’s death that make you feel like you’re reliving the event?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yes. Honestly, that's exactly what brings me in here to see you in the first place. It’s just... it’s completely overwhelming. I keep getting hit, just completely out of nowhere, with this... this scene of my cousin lying there on that pavement. And it's not like I'm just remembering it like a normal, bad memory, you know? It's like I am physically ripped right back into that exact second in time. \n\nI can hear the shouting all over again—the exact pitch of the voices, the chaos, the panic—and I can see those police lights flashing, just bouncing off the brick on the side of the fellowship hall. It's so vivid. And the craziest part is, it doesn't even just happen when I'm alone at night or something. I could literally just be standing in the church office. Just standing there by the desk, trying to act completely normal, maybe looking over a scripture reading for Sunday or talking to the church secretary about the bulletin, and then suddenly... the office is just gone. The present is gone. I'm right back on that pavement, seeing him there, hearing the shouting, seeing those lights. \n\nI try so hard to just blink it away and keep a straight face, because people are looking at me and I'm supposed to be their pastor, I have to hold it together and be the strong one. But I'm just reliving that horrible, horrible night all over again, right there in the middle of a Tuesday afternoon. It just takes over everything, no matter how hard I try to stay in the present moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling especially irritable or having outbursts of anger lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I really have. And it’s... it’s honestly so out of character for me, but lately, it’s just like I have absolutely zero patience. Zero. My fuse is just completely gone. I’ve actually been snapping in our ministry meetings over completely stupid, small things. \n\nLike, just last week we were in a planning committee for the upcoming youth retreat, just a standard Tuesday night meeting with the deacons and some of my volunteer youth leaders. And someone asked a completely innocent question about the transportation schedule, or maybe the budget for snacks, I can't even remember what it was, but I just... I completely lost it. I raised my voice at them. I was practically yelling at these deacons and youth leaders, people who are just volunteering their free time to help me, and I was talking to them like they were the enemy. Like they were actively trying to attack me or sabotage the ministry. \n\nThe whole room just went dead silent, and everyone was just staring at me. I mean, I'm the pastor, I'm supposed to be the calm, steady one, the one who brings peace to the room. And afterward... man, I just hate how fast I went there. I hate it so much. It was like going from completely fine to absolute rage in a fraction of a second. I had to go sit in my office after they left and just put my head in my hands, because I don't even know where this anger is coming from. It’s like my nerves are just completely raw, and the slightest bit of pushback or confusion just sets me off. I’ve had to apologize so many times lately, and I just know they have to be looking at me differently now, wondering why their youth pastor is suddenly acting so hostile over a simple logistical question. It just feels like I can't stop the anger before it spills out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that used to be meaningful to you, like leading your youth group or preparing your sermons?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it’s like everything just lost its color completely. You brought up preparing sermons, and... man, I used to love preaching. I really did. I would spend hours on a Wednesday night just digging into the text, trying to find the perfect illustration, getting so fired up to share it with the youth group. It was my absolute favorite thing. But now? I just stare at my laptop screen. I type a few bullet points and I just... I mostly just go through the motions. I stand up there and say the words, but there's nothing behind them. \n\nAnd it’s not just the formal ministry stuff, either. It’s literally everything. Like, I’ve played the keys since I was ten years old. I used to get up there with the worship band on Sunday mornings, or even just sit in the empty sanctuary on a Thursday afternoon and just play for hours. Just me and the piano, feeling so connected to God and to the music. Now, I don't even want to look at the keyboard. I've been asking the other worship leader to cover all my sets because I just can't bring myself to press the keys. \n\nSame with taking pictures. I used to always have my camera around my neck, always snapping candids of the kids, the retreats, the baptisms... I loved capturing those moments. But my camera has just been sitting in its bag in my office since that night. I haven't touched it once. \n\nAnd... and even just hanging out. We used to have this pickup game, running with the guys on the court behind the church. It was my favorite way to blow off steam and connect with the older teens outside of a formal church setting. We'd play until the streetlights came on, just laughing and talking trash. But now, when they text me to come out and play, I just make up an excuse. I avoid it altogether. I avoid all of it. \n\nI don't know how else to explain it other than saying that... none of it feels alive in me anymore. It’s all just dead. I'm just a ghost walking through the halls of that church, doing what I'm supposed to do because I'm on staff, but the joy, the passion... it's just completely gone. I just can't seem to care about any of it the way I used to, and it breaks my heart because this was my whole life, you know? This was my calling. And now I just... I just don't have anything left to give to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that it's harder for you to focus, make decisions, or think clearly lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It's... it's honestly terrifying how much my brain just feels like it's full of thick fog right now. I was actually just thinking about this the other day because it’s becoming a real liability for my job. \n\nWhen I sit down to write a sermon... well, I mentioned earlier how much I used to love doing that, right? I used to be able to just dive in, connect all these different theological concepts, pull from my undergrad classes, and just outline a message with no problem. But now? I sit there at my desk in the church office, I open up my laptop, and my mind just completely blanks out. It’s like someone literally just pulled the plug on my brain. There is just nothing there. Just static. \n\nAnd it’s not even just the heavy, deep stuff that takes a lot of mental energy, either. It’s the incredibly simple, mundane things. Like just answering church emails. I’ll open my inbox, and there will be a message from one of the youth parents asking a basic question about the permission slips for the fall retreat, or maybe an email from the senior pastor about the order of service. Just totally standard stuff that I've handled a thousand times since I got ordained. I will literally sit there and read the exact same line five or six times in a row, and I am just not taking in a single thing it says. I see the letters, I know they form words, but the meaning just doesn't register in my head at all. It’s incredibly frustrating. I used to be so sharp, you know? I could juggle the youth group schedule, volunteer coordination, counseling sessions, all of it, and never drop a ball. Now, I can barely figure out how to reply to an email about a pizza party without my brain hitting a massive brick wall. I just sit there staring at the blinking cursor, totally paralyzed, unable to form a single coherent thought or make a basic decision about what to type back. It takes me an hour to do what used to take me two minutes, and it is just completely exhausting trying to force my brain to work through this sludge every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, even things that used to be really meaningful to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... I'll just make up excuses to get out of it. Because... well, like I said earlier, none of it feels alive in me anymore. I can sit down at the piano, or I can look at my camera sitting in its bag, or I can hear the guys playing basketball outside my office window, and there is just zero spark. Nothing. It's like the part of my soul that used to connect with those things, the part that used to feel joy and passion, is just completely numb now. I'll just stare at my sermon notes or my keyboard and just feel this heavy, dead weight in my chest. It's incredibly isolating, honestly, because my whole life was built around these things, and now I just feel completely disconnected from all of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in activities that used to be meaningful to you, like leading your youth group or preparing your sermons?\n\nPatient: I... Doc, I feel like we keep circling back to this, and I don't know how else to explain it, but yes. Yes, absolutely. Like I was just saying a minute ago, and even before that... it's all just gone. I... look, Doctor, I don't want to be disrespectful, but I really feel like I'm just repeating myself over and over here. We've talked about this. I've said this. I don't know how to make it any clearer.
